1. Executive Summary
This document delivers a bank of 1,000 original multiple-choice items engineered to match the style, cognitive profile,
and content balance of the official DECA Cluster Exams used in DECA's Competitive Events Program. It is intended
as a reference seed for generating a 40,000-item library, so exhaustive coverage of every instructional area and the
full range of performance-indicator difficulty tiers has been prioritized over strict proportional mimicry of any single
form.
The official blueprint is published by MBA Research and Curriculum Center, which develops DECA's exams. All four
supported career-cluster exams are 100-item, multiple-choice tests built from a 30,000+ item bank and prorated
across performance indicators so each instructional area appears in proportion to its share of the national Business
Administration standards.1 True/false items, combination items (e.g., "A & B are correct"), and "all/none of the above"
options are not used; the four clusters are Marketing, Business Management & Administration, Finance, and
Hospitality & Tourism.1,2
These design principles were extracted directly from the public blueprint and sample exams, then encoded into
item-writing rules applied during generation. Each item below includes the stem, four parallel options (A–D), one best
answer, a rationale for the correct option, and a rationale for each of the three distractors explaining why it is plausible
but incorrect.

2. Methodology & Evidence Base
2.1 Sources reviewed
The design model rests on the following public DECA and MBA Research materials:
  MBA Research, DECA Exam Specifications 2024–2025 — the official blueprint giving the 100-item structure,
prohibited item formats, performance-indicator levels, and per-instructional-area question counts for all four cluster
exams.1
  MBA Research, Marketing Career Cluster sample exam (posted for DECA) — a public 100-item sample with
answer rationales used to infer stem style, cognitive demand, and distractor construction.2
  DECA chapter practice sets (BHS, Novi, Enloe) and public practice banks — used to triangulate recurring item
patterns, vocabulary density, and scenario style across clusters.3,4
  DECA Direct, A Student Guide to Mastering DECA's Role-plays — confirms the cluster → instructional area →
performance-indicator hierarchy and the three planning levels.5
  decaplus.org, Online Practice Exams — confirms each exam is a 100-item, industry-validated, multiple-choice test
and that content becomes more specialized to the cluster as members advance.6
2.2 What was extracted
From the blueprint, the exact per-instructional-area question counts at the District (A) level were extracted for each
cluster and used as the allocation skeleton for generation. From the sample exams, the following were inferred by
direct inspection: average stem length (~20–25 words), the mix of direct-definition and short-scenario stems,
business-education vocabulary density, the absence of "all/none of the above" and "Which is NOT…" structures, the
parallelism of option grammar and length, and the predominantly knowledge/comprehension-plus-application
cognitive profile.
2.3 Evidence vs. inference
Conclusions labeled supported are stated explicitly in the MBA Research specifications (e.g., 100-item
multiple-choice format; no true/false; no combination items; prorated distribution across performance indicators; the
three planning levels). Conclusions labeled inferred derive from recurring patterns in the public sample exams (e.g.,
the ~60/35/5 cognitive mix, stem-length range, distractor near-miss construction, and randomized correct-answer
position).

DECA Cluster Exam — Reference Question Bank (Original Items)

3. Reverse-Engineered Design Model
3.1 Supported design principles (from the official blueprint)
  Format: every exam is 100 multiple-choice items; exactly four options (A–D); exactly one best answer.
  Prohibited formats: no true/false items; no combination items (e.g., "A & B are correct"); no "all/none of the
above."1
  Distribution: items are prorated across all eligible performance indicators so each instructional area appears in
proportion to its share of the national standards; actual counts may vary slightly.1
  Planning levels: performance indicators span three tiers — Prerequisite, Career-Sustaining, and Specialist — with
district/state exams drawing from the lower tiers and national (ICDC) adding specialist-level items.1
  Discrimination: exams are designed for test discrimination — a broad score spread with lower overall means — to
validly separate top performers.1
  Bank size & independence: the item bank exceeds 30,000 items; exams are fully independent with little or no
overlapping question use.1
  Cluster structure: each cluster exam includes the Business Administration Core; the four clusters are Marketing,
Business Management & Administration, Finance, and Hospitality & Tourism.2,5
3.2 Inferred item-writing principles (from sample-exam patterns)
  Stem length: ~20–25 words on average, ranging from one-line definitions to ~60-word scenarios; both direct and
scenario stems appear regularly.2
  Cognitive demand: predominantly Knowledge/Comprehension plus Application — items name a
term/concept/best practice or describe a workplace situation and ask for identification of the best answer; analysis
and judgment are a minority.
  Vocabulary: business-education register recognizable to a high-school marketing student (e.g., fiduciary, SWOT,
brand insistence, channel management, scanner fraud, diminishing returns, intangible outputs) — not
MBA/case-level.2
  Negatives: the explicit "Which of the following is NOT…" stem is avoided; cautionary scenario phrasing (e.g., "does
not want to over-distribute," "fails to warn") is authentic and common.
  Distractors: four plausible near-miss options targeting common student misconceptions; grammar, length, and
form parallel across options; correct-answer position randomized.2
  Tone: formal, objective, third-person, instructional; realistic small-business/retail/service/hospitality contexts with
varied personal names.
3.3 Official District-level instructional-area distributions (per 100-item exam)
These counts, taken from the MBA Research specifications,1 were scaled to ~250 items per cluster during
generation. Areas with zero items at district level (e.g., Knowledge Management in Hospitality) were still seeded with
a minimum of three items so the reference bank can template them for the 40,000-item expansion.

4. Generation Rules & Quality Controls
The following rules were enforced for every item in this bank. They were derived directly from Section 3 and applied
uniformly across all four clusters.
1. Four options (A–D); exactly one best answer. No "all/none of the above," no true/false, no combination answers.
2. No "Which of the following is NOT…" stems; cautionary scenario phrasing is permitted and authentic.
3. Entirely original wording — no official DECA/MBA Research stem, option, or proper name is copied verbatim.
4. Grammar, length, and form are parallel across the four options within each item.
5. Correct-answer position is randomized across the bank — target ~25% each for A, B, C, D.
6. Cognitive mix target: ~60% conceptual (recall/recognition/understanding), ~35% application (short scenarios), ~5%
analysis/judgment.
7. Difficulty mix target: ~35% Easy, ~45% Medium, ~20% Hard.
8. Vocabulary stays at the high-school business-education level; distractors target common student misconceptions,
not obscure technical traps.
9. Every instructional area in every cluster receives coverage; small areas are seeded with a minimum number of
items so they can serve as templates.
10. Each item is a self-contained template — stem, four options, correct answer, correct-option rationale, and three
distractor rationales — for downstream replication.
4.1 Validation checks performed
  All four JSON files re-parse cleanly; total = 1,000 items.
  No duplicate stems within or across clusters; no two items test the identical fact.
  Every item has exactly four distinct, parallel options, one correct answer, one rationale, and three distractor
rationales.
  Zero items use "all/none of the above," true/false, combination answers, or the "Which is NOT…" structure.
  Correct-answer position and difficulty distributions verified against targets (see Section 5).

5.5 Performance-indicator coverage
DECA organizes each cluster's content into instructional areas, each containing many official performance indicators
(PIs) across the Prerequisite, Career-Sustaining, and Specialist tiers.5,7 Because this bank seeds a 40,000-item
library, every official instructional area is represented, and within each area the items target a spread of DECA-style
performance-indicator focuses rather than a single fact.
Coverage claim: all official instructional areas for all four clusters are covered, with performance-indicator focuses
phrased in DECA's style and aligned to the public PI lists. The focuses are modeled on (not transcribed from) the
official indicators.

Page 12
6. How to Use This Bank as a Generation Reference
Because this bank seeds a 40,000-item library, each item is structured as a reusable template. When expanding:
  Preserve the schema (id, instructional_area, performance_indicator_focus, cognitive_level, difficulty, stem, options,
correct, rationale, distractor_rationales) so downstream items remain queryable and balanced.
  Within an instructional area, hold the performance-indicator focus constant while varying the scenario surface
(industry context, named character, quantities) to produce parallel-but-distinct items at the same cognitive level.
  To raise difficulty, escalate the cognitive level (Recall → Understanding → Application → Analysis) and lengthen
the stem to a scenario; to lower it, use a one-line definition stem with near-miss distractors drawn from adjacent
concepts.
  Keep distractor rationales diagnostic: each should name the specific misconception it exploits, so generators can
systematically vary the misconception rather than the topic.
  Re-check Section 5's distribution targets after every batch to prevent drift in answer-position balance, difficulty mix,
and instructional-area coverage.

DECA Cluster Exam — Reference Question Bank (Original Items)

7. Question Bank — Marketing Cluster (M001–M250)
250 original items, organized sequentially by ID. Each item shows the stem, four parallel options, the correct answer,
and rationales for the correct option and each distractor.
What term describes the process of collecting, recording, and analyzing data about problems relating to the
marketing of goods and services?
M001  |  Marketing  |  Marketing-Information Management  |  Recall  |  Easy  |  Answer: D
A. Inventory control
B. Financial forecasting
C. Personnel scheduling
D. Marketing research
Correct (D): Marketing research is defined as the systematic gathering, recording, and analysis of data about marketing
problems.
A — why plausible but wrong: Inventory control tracks stock levels, not marketing information.
B — why plausible but wrong: Financial forecasting deals with predicting revenue or costs, not marketing data.
C — why plausible but wrong: Personnel scheduling relates to staffing, not marketing research.
A candle company surveys its own customers by phone to learn about scent preferences. This survey is an
example of what kind of data?
M002  |  Marketing  |  Marketing-Information Management  |  Understanding  |  Easy  |  Answer: D
A. Historical census data
B. Syndicated data
C. Secondary data
D. Primary data
Correct (D): Primary data is collected firsthand by a business for a specific research purpose, such as a phone survey the
company conducts itself.
A — why plausible but wrong: Historical census data is government-collected information, not the company's own survey.
B — why plausible but wrong: Syndicated data is gathered by outside research firms and sold to multiple companies.
C — why plausible but wrong: Secondary data was already collected by someone else for another purpose, unlike this original
survey.
Why do marketing managers rely on a well-organized marketing-information system rather than relying on
personal opinions alone?
M003  |  Marketing  |  Marketing-Information Management  |  Understanding  |  Easy  |  Answer: A
A. It provides consistent, timely data to support sound decisions
B. It guarantees that every new product will be profitable
C. It eliminates the need for any employee training
D. It reduces the amount of paperwork required for tax filings
Correct (A): A marketing-information system gathers and organizes data continuously so managers can make informed, timely
decisions instead of guessing.
B — why plausible but wrong: No information system can guarantee profitability; it only improves decision quality.
C — why plausible but wrong: Such systems do not remove the need for training employees on how to use the data.
D — why plausible but wrong: Tax paperwork is unrelated to the purpose of a marketing-information system.


--- PAGE 14 ---
DECA Cluster Exam — Reference Question Bank (Original Items)
Page 14
A pet-supply retailer wants in-depth, open-ended opinions from a small number of loyal customers about a
new subscription box. Which research method best fits this goal?
M004  |  Marketing  |  Marketing-Information Management  |  Application  |  Medium  |  Answer: C
A. Census of all households
B. Point-of-sale scanner audit
C. Focus group
D. Random mail survey
Correct (C): A focus group brings together a small number of participants for guided, open-ended discussion, ideal for gathering
in-depth opinions.
A — why plausible but wrong: A census of all households would be far too large and costly for this narrow, in-depth goal.
B — why plausible but wrong: A scanner audit records purchase transactions, not open-ended customer opinions.
D — why plausible but wrong: A random mail survey gathers broad, structured responses, not in-depth discussion.
A bakery's sales data show that muffin sales spike every Friday but the owner does not know why. What
should the owner do first to use this marketing information effectively?
M005  |  Marketing  |  Marketing-Information Management  |  Application  |  Medium  |  Answer: A
A. Investigate the cause of the Friday sales pattern before acting
B. Raise muffin prices on all days equally
C. Ignore the pattern since it may be a coincidence
D. Discontinue the muffin product line immediately
Correct (A): Effective use of marketing information requires interpreting the data and investigating causes before making
decisions.
B — why plausible but wrong: Raising prices on all days ignores the specific Friday pattern the data reveals.
C — why plausible but wrong: Ignoring a clear, recurring pattern wastes valuable marketing information.
D — why plausible but wrong: Discontinuing a popular product without understanding the cause would be a poor, premature
decision.
What is the primary purpose of a marketing-information system (MIS)?
M006  |  Marketing  |  Marketing-Information Management  |  Recall  |  Easy  |  Answer: A
A. To supply accurate, timely data for marketing decisions
B. To negotiate contracts with suppliers
C. To manufacture products more efficiently
D. To process employee payroll and benefits
Correct (A): A marketing-information system exists to continuously gather, sort, and deliver relevant data for marketing
decision-making.
B — why plausible but wrong: Supplier negotiation is a purchasing activity, separate from an MIS.
C — why plausible but wrong: Manufacturing efficiency belongs to operations, not marketing information.
D — why plausible but wrong: Payroll processing is a human resources function, not a marketing-information system function.
When a soft-drink company selects a small group of consumers to represent the opinions of the entire
market, this group is called the:
M007  |  Marketing  |  Marketing-Information Management  |  Understanding  |  Medium  |  Answer: A
A. Sample
B. Census
C. Population
D. Panel budget
Correct (A): A sample is a smaller group selected from a larger population to represent that population's characteristics in
research.
B — why plausible but wrong: A census collects data from every member of the population, not a subset.
C — why plausible but wrong: The population is the entire group being studied, not the smaller selected group.
D — why plausible but wrong: A panel budget is not a recognized research term for a representative group.


--- PAGE 15 ---
DECA Cluster Exam — Reference Question Bank (Original Items)
Page 15
A large retail chain stores years of point-of-sale transactions in one central digital repository so analysts can
later mine it for trends. This repository is best described as a:
M008  |  Marketing  |  Marketing-Information Management  |  Recognition  |  Medium  |  Answer: C
A. Focus group transcript
B. Organizational chart
C. Data warehouse
D. Sales quota sheet
Correct (C): A data warehouse is a centralized repository that stores large volumes of historical data for later analysis and
reporting.
A — why plausible but wrong: A focus group transcript records qualitative discussion notes, not transactional data storage.
B — why plausible but wrong: An organizational chart shows reporting relationships, unrelated to data storage.
D — why plausible but wrong: A sales quota sheet lists performance targets, not a data storage system.
A market researcher discovers that combining two datasets would reveal individual customers' identities
even though the survey promised anonymity. What is the most ethical action for the researcher to take?
M009  |  Marketing  |  Marketing-Information Management  |  Judgment  |  Hard  |  Answer: C
A. Publish the combined dataset since it improves analysis
B. Sell the combined dataset to a third-party marketer
C. Keep the datasets separate to protect respondent anonymity
D. Share the identities privately with the sales team only
Correct (C): Ethical research practice requires protecting the confidentiality promised to respondents, so the researcher must
keep identifying data separate.
A — why plausible but wrong: Publishing data that exposes identities breaks the promise of anonymity made to respondents.
B — why plausible but wrong: Selling data that reveals identities without consent is an unethical breach of privacy.
D — why plausible but wrong: Sharing identities internally still violates the anonymity guarantee given to participants.
Which technology allows a grocery chain to automatically capture what customers buy every time they
check out?
M010  |  Marketing  |  Marketing-Information Management  |  Recognition  |  Medium  |  Answer: C
A. Employee time clock
B. Payroll direct-deposit system
C. Point-of-sale scanning system
D. Organizational flowchart
Correct (C): Point-of-sale scanning systems capture item-level purchase data automatically at checkout, feeding it into the
marketing-information system.
A — why plausible but wrong: An employee time clock tracks work hours, unrelated to customer purchases.
B — why plausible but wrong: A payroll direct-deposit system handles employee pay, not sales data.
D — why plausible but wrong: An organizational flowchart shows staffing structure, not purchase data collection.
A furniture store wants to know why sales of a certain sofa model dropped last quarter. Which step of the
marketing-research process should the store complete first?
M011  |  Marketing  |  Marketing-Information Management  |  Understanding  |  Easy  |  Answer: B
A. Select the sample population
B. Define the problem to be researched
C. Analyze the collected data
D. Implement the research findings
Correct (B): The first step in the marketing-research process is clearly defining the problem, which guides every later step.
A — why plausible but wrong: Selecting a sample comes after the problem has been clearly defined.
C — why plausible but wrong: Analyzing data occurs after data has been collected, later in the process.
D — why plausible but wrong: Implementing findings happens only after research is designed, conducted, and analyzed.


--- PAGE 16 ---
DECA Cluster Exam — Reference Question Bank (Original Items)
Page 16
An online retailer uses software to track which web pages customers view before abandoning their shopping
carts. This use of technology primarily helps the retailer to:
M012  |  Marketing  |  Marketing-Information Management  |  Application  |  Medium  |  Answer: C
A. Print shipping labels for orders
B. Calculate corporate income tax owed
C. Identify patterns in customer browsing behavior
D. Set employee vacation schedules
Correct (C): Tracking software captures behavioral data so marketers can identify patterns, such as where shoppers abandon
their carts.
A — why plausible but wrong: Shipping-label printing is a fulfillment task, not customer-behavior analysis.
B — why plausible but wrong: Tax calculation is a finance function, not a use of browsing-tracking technology.
D — why plausible but wrong: Vacation scheduling is a human-resources task unrelated to browsing analytics.
What is the main benefit of storing customer information in a searchable database rather than in scattered
paper files?
M013  |  Marketing  |  Marketing-Information Management  |  Recall  |  Easy  |  Answer: C
A. It eliminates the need for customer service staff
B. It automatically increases product prices
C. It allows quick, organized retrieval of customer data
D. It replaces the need for a marketing plan
Correct (C): A database organizes information so it can be searched and retrieved quickly, improving efficiency over scattered
paper records.
A — why plausible but wrong: Databases support customer service; they do not eliminate the need for staff.
B — why plausible but wrong: A database has no effect on setting product prices.
D — why plausible but wrong: A database supports planning but does not replace the need for a marketing plan.
A shoe company wants industry-wide sales trends but does not have time or budget to conduct its own
study. Which source would most efficiently meet this need?
M014  |  Marketing  |  Marketing-Information Management  |  Application  |  Medium  |  Answer: C
A. A single customer's complaint letter
B. A random informal conversation with one shopper
C. A published trade-association industry report
D. An internal employee suggestion box
Correct (C): Published trade-association reports are secondary data sources that provide broad industry trends quickly and
inexpensively.
A — why plausible but wrong: One complaint letter reflects a single customer's view, not overall industry data.
B — why plausible but wrong: A single informal conversation is not a reliable, representative data source.
D — why plausible but wrong: A suggestion box collects internal employee ideas, not industry-wide sales trends.
A market report shows a correlation between ice cream sales and sunscreen sales but a beginning analyst
assumes one causes the other. What is the flaw in this analyst's reasoning?
M015  |  Marketing  |  Marketing-Information Management  |  Application  |  Hard  |  Answer: B
A. Correlation always proves direct causation between variables
B. Both variables likely respond to a third factor, hot weather
C. Sunscreen sales figures are always inaccurate
D. Sales data cannot ever be compared across products
Correct (B): This is a classic case of a confounding variable; hot weather likely drives both ice cream and sunscreen sales, not
one causing the other.
A — why plausible but wrong: This restates the analyst's flawed assumption rather than identifying the actual error.
C — why plausible but wrong: There is no indication that the sunscreen sales figures themselves are inaccurate.
D — why plausible but wrong: Comparing sales data across products is a valid and common analytical practice.

scenario.

C. Let each manager spend independently without any coordination
D. Facilitate a structured discussion focused on shared business goals
Correct (D): A structured discussion centered on shared organizational goals helps both parties find common ground and reach
a sustainable resolution.
A — why plausible but wrong: Avoiding the issue allows tension to persist and likely worsens over time.
B — why plausible but wrong: A top-down decision without input may resolve the immediate issue but can breed resentment
and repeat conflicts.
C — why plausible but wrong: Uncoordinated independent spending could worsen inefficiency and conflict.

A. Assumption that all clients share identical business practices

C. Awareness and respect for different cultural communication norms
D. Avoidance of any communication with clients from unfamiliar cultures
Correct (C): Cultural sensitivity involves recognizing and respecting differences in communication styles and customs among
diverse clients.
A — why plausible but wrong: Assuming identical practices across cultures ignores real and important differences.
B — why plausible but wrong: Insisting others adapt entirely to one's own customs disregards cultural differences.
D — why plausible but wrong: Avoiding communication would prevent effective business relationships from forming.

A candle shop has fixed costs of $2,000 per month and sells each candle for $20 with a variable cost of $10
per candle. How many candles must the shop sell each month to break even?
M114  |  Marketing  |  Financial Analysis  |  Application  |  Medium  |  Answer: A
A. 200 candles
B. 400 candles
C. 20 candles
D. 100 candles
Correct (A): Break-even units equal fixed costs divided by contribution margin per unit ($20 minus $10 equals $10); $2,000
divided by $10 equals 200 candles.
B — why plausible but wrong: This figure incorrectly doubles the correct break-even quantity.
C — why plausible but wrong: This figure results from dividing fixed costs by the total price and variable cost combined, an
incorrect method.
D — why plausible but wrong: This figure results from dividing fixed costs by the selling price rather than by the contribution
margin.

D. To assess the business's ability to repay the loan based on financial health
Correct (D): Lenders review financial statements to evaluate a business's revenue, expenses, and overall financial health before
extending credit.
A — why plausible but wrong: Hiring decisions are an internal business matter, not something lenders evaluate for loans.
B — why plausible but wrong: Advertising slogans are a marketing decision unrelated to a loan approval process.
C — why plausible but wrong: Personal preferences are unrelated to a lender's assessment of creditworthiness.

A. Brand equity

C. Cash flow
D. Product positioning

A — why plausible but wrong: Brand equity refers to the value associated with a brand name, unrelated to cash timing issues.
B — why plausible but wrong: Market segmentation divides customers into groups, unrelated to cash timing problems.
D — why plausible but wrong: Product positioning concerns brand image in customers' minds, not cash-flow timing.

assuming no risk of nonpayment?
M120  |  Marketing  |  Financial Analysis  |  Understanding  |  Hard  |  Answer: C

B. Future payments are always illegal in business contracts
C. Money received today can be invested to earn returns over that year
D. Money loses all of its purchasing power after exactly one year

sooner.

A company spends $5,000 on an email marketing campaign that generates $15,000 in additional profit. What
is the return on investment (ROI) of this campaign?
M122  |  Marketing  |  Financial Analysis  |  Application  |  Medium  |  Answer: A
A. 200 percent
B. 300 percent
C. 66 percent
D. 33 percent
Correct (A): ROI equals (gain from investment minus cost of investment) divided by cost of investment: ($15,000 - $5,000) /
$5,000 = 200 percent.
B — why plausible but wrong: This figure incorrectly divides total profit by cost without subtracting the initial investment first.
C — why plausible but wrong: This figure does not correctly apply the ROI formula to the given numbers.
D — why plausible but wrong: This figure incorrectly divides the cost by the total profit rather than calculating net gain over
cost.

B — why plausible but wrong: A purchase order is a formal document sent to a supplier authorizing a shipment, not a
customer request note.
C — why plausible but wrong: A balance sheet is a financial statement, unrelated to recording customer product requests.
D — why plausible but wrong: An invoice is a bill for goods or services already provided, unrelated to tracking unmet requests.
While a customer explains exactly what features they need in a laptop, a salesperson listens carefully and
takes brief notes before making a recommendation. This behavior primarily helps the salesperson:
M170  |  Marketing  |  Selling  |  Application  |  Medium  |  Answer: A
A. Accurately match a product recommendation to the customer's stated needs
B. Increase the store's overall advertising budget
C. Eliminate the need for a product demonstration
D. Avoid having to complete any sales paperwork
Correct (A): Careful listening ensures the salesperson understands the customer's specific needs, leading to a more accurate
and relevant recommendation.
B — why plausible but wrong: Listening skills are unrelated to setting the advertising budget.
C — why plausible but wrong: A product demonstration is often still valuable even after listening to stated needs.
D — why plausible but wrong: Listening to a customer does not eliminate necessary sales paperwork.
The path a product takes from the producer to the final consumer, including any intermediaries, is known as
a:
M171  |  Marketing  |  Channel Management  |  Recall  |  Easy  |  Answer: D
A. Marketing budget
B. Balance sheet
C. Break-even chart
D. Channel of distribution
Correct (D): A channel of distribution is the path, including any intermediaries such as wholesalers or retailers, that a product
follows from producer to consumer.
A — why plausible but wrong: A marketing budget outlines planned spending, unrelated to distribution routing.
B — why plausible but wrong: A balance sheet is a financial statement, unrelated to the flow of goods to market.
C — why plausible but wrong: A break-even chart shows cost-revenue relationships, unrelated to distribution paths.

Before adopting real-time inventory software, a retailer took two weeks to identify which products were

M208  |  Marketing  |  Information Management  |  Analysis  |  Hard  |  Answer: D

B. A guaranteed decrease in the price paid to suppliers
C. Elimination of the need for any human oversight of inventory

Correct (D): Faster, automated identification of low-stock items allows the retailer to reorder sooner, reducing the chance of lost
sales due to stockouts.

B — why plausible but wrong: Faster inventory tracking does not directly determine supplier pricing.
C — why plausible but wrong: Automated flagging still typically requires human review and decision-making, not full
elimination of oversight.

M220  |  Marketing  |  Pricing  |  Understanding  |  Medium  |  Answer: C
A. An update to the company's holiday schedule
B. A change in the company's office furniture
C. An increase in production and material costs
D. A new employee parking policy

A retailer prices a jacket at $49.99 instead of $50.00 because research shows customers perceive it as a
meaningfully better deal. This pricing tactic is known as:
M221  |  Marketing  |  Pricing  |  Application  |  Medium  |  Answer: A
A. Psychological pricing
B. Cost-plus pricing
C. Penetration pricing
D. Break-even pricing
Correct (A): Psychological pricing uses prices such as $49.99 rather than round numbers because customers often perceive
them as significantly lower.
B — why plausible but wrong: Cost-plus pricing adds a fixed markup to cost, unrelated to perception-based pricing.
C — why plausible but wrong: Penetration pricing sets an initially low price to gain market share quickly, a different strategy.
D — why plausible but wrong: Break-even pricing sets price to cover exact costs, not to exploit customer price perception.
A boutique buys a scarf for $10 and sells it for $25. What is the dollar amount of markup on this item?
M222  |  Marketing  |  Pricing  |  Application  |  Medium  |  Answer: A
A. $15
B. $35
C. $25
D. $10
Correct (A): Markup is calculated by subtracting the cost ($10) from the selling price ($25), resulting in a $15 markup.
B — why plausible but wrong: This figure incorrectly adds the cost and selling price together rather than subtracting.
C — why plausible but wrong: This figure is simply the selling price, not the markup amount.
D — why plausible but wrong: This figure is simply the cost, not the markup amount.

A tech company launches a new smartphone at a very high initial price to maximize profit from early
adopters before gradually lowering the price over time. This strategy is known as:
M223  |  Marketing  |  Pricing  |  Understanding  |  Medium  |  Answer: D
A. Penetration pricing
B. Bundle pricing
C. Loss-leader pricing
D. Price skimming
Correct (D): Price skimming sets a high initial price to capture maximum revenue from early adopters before lowering the price
to attract broader markets.
A — why plausible but wrong: Penetration pricing sets a low initial price to gain market share quickly, the opposite approach.
B — why plausible but wrong: Bundle pricing combines multiple products for one combined price, unrelated to this scenario.
C — why plausible but wrong: Loss-leader pricing prices one item very low to draw customers who then buy other products.
A candle maker calculates the exact price needed so that total revenue from expected sales will just cover all
fixed and variable costs, with no profit or loss. This pricing method is known as:
M224  |  Marketing  |  Pricing  |  Application  |  Medium  |  Answer: D
A. Psychological pricing
B. Prestige pricing
C. Prestige skimming
D. Break-even pricing
Correct (D): Break-even pricing sets price at the level where total revenue exactly equals total costs, resulting in neither profit
nor loss.
A — why plausible but wrong: Psychological pricing uses perception-based figures like $9.99, not a strict cost-covering
calculation.
B — why plausible but wrong: Prestige pricing sets deliberately high prices to convey luxury or exclusivity, unrelated to
covering costs exactly.
C — why plausible but wrong: This is not a recognized standard pricing strategy term.

This coordinated pricing practice is best classified as:
M225  |  Marketing  |  Pricing  |  Judgment  |  Hard  |  Answer: B
A. Legal competitive pricing
B. Illegal price fixing
C. Standard psychological pricing
D. Acceptable price skimming
Correct (B): Price fixing occurs when competitors illegally agree to set prices together rather than competing independently,
violating antitrust laws.
A — why plausible but wrong: Legitimate competitive pricing involves independent decisions, not secret coordination among
competitors.
C — why plausible but wrong: Psychological pricing relates to perception-based pricing figures, not illegal coordination.
D — why plausible but wrong: Price skimming is a legal strategy for pricing new products high, unrelated to this illegal
coordination.

strategic planning.
C — why plausible but wrong: Order fulfillment involves completing customer orders, an operational task rather than strategic
planning.

A legally binding agreement between two or more parties that includes an offer, acceptance, and
consideration is called a
B001  |  Business Management & Administration  |  Business Law  |  Recall  |  Easy  |  Answer: A
A. contract
B. memorandum
C. invoice
D. prospectus
Correct (A): A contract is a legally enforceable agreement formed through offer, acceptance, and consideration.
B — why plausible but wrong: A memorandum is an internal document for communication, not necessarily a legally binding
agreement.
C — why plausible but wrong: An invoice is a billing record, not a binding agreement with mutual obligations.
D — why plausible but wrong: A prospectus is a document describing an investment offering, not a general contract.

A. trademark
B. business license
C. stock certificate
D. noncompete agreement
Correct (B): A business license grants legal permission from a government authority to operate a business in a given location.
A — why plausible but wrong: A trademark protects a brand name or logo, unrelated to permission to operate.
C — why plausible but wrong: A stock certificate proves ownership of company shares, unrelated to operating permission.
D — why plausible but wrong: A noncompete agreement restricts an employee from working for competitors, unrelated to
business licensing.

A. trademark
B. copyright

B012  |  Business Management & Administration  |  Business Law  |  Understanding  |  Medium  |  Answer: A

B — why plausible but wrong: Refusing further communication is unprofessional and fails to resolve the situation.
C — why plausible but wrong: Publicly blaming a customer is unprofessional and damages the company's reputation.
When the price of a popular smartphone drops significantly, the quantity consumers are willing to buy
typically increases. This relationship illustrates the law of
B037  |  Business Management & Administration  |  Economics  |  Understanding  |  Easy  |  Answer: B
A. supply
B. demand
C. diminishing returns
D. comparative advantage
Correct (B): The law of demand states that as price decreases, the quantity demanded generally increases, all else equal.
A — why plausible but wrong: The law of supply describes how quantity supplied changes with price from the seller's
perspective, not the buyer's.
C — why plausible but wrong: Diminishing returns describes decreasing output gains from additional input in production,
unrelated to consumer price response.
D — why plausible but wrong: Comparative advantage explains why entities specialize in production, unrelated to
price-demand relationships.

B038  |  Business Management & Administration  |  Economics  |  Recall  |  Easy  |  Answer: B
A. surplus
B. scarcity
C. inflation
D. recession

A — why plausible but wrong: A surplus occurs when supply exceeds demand at a given price, a market condition rather than
the basic economic problem.
C — why plausible but wrong: Inflation is a general rise in prices over time, unrelated to the fundamental scarcity problem.
D — why plausible but wrong: A recession is a period of economic decline, unrelated to the basic scarcity concept.

On a balance sheet, the equation Assets equals Liabilities plus Owner's Equity demonstrates that
B075  |  Business Management & Administration  |  Financial Analysis  |  Understanding  |  Easy  |  Answer: C
A. all expenses must equal all revenues
B. cash flow always equals net income
C. everything a business owns is financed by debt, owner investment, or both
D. profit is the same as revenue

D — why plausible but wrong: Profit and revenue are different figures; revenue minus expenses equals profit.
A candle maker has fixed costs of $2,000 per month and sells each candle for $10, with a variable cost of $6
per candle. How many candles must be sold each month to break even?
B076  |  Business Management & Administration  |  Financial Analysis  |  Application  |  Medium  |  Answer: D
A. 200
B. 333
C. 800
D. 500
Correct (D): Break-even quantity equals fixed costs divided by contribution margin per unit ($2,000 / $4 = 500 units).
A — why plausible but wrong: 200 results from dividing fixed costs by the selling price instead of the contribution margin.
B — why plausible but wrong: 333 results from an incorrect contribution margin calculation.
C — why plausible but wrong: 800 overestimates the required unit sales by using an incorrect denominator.

B. 8%

A. debt-to-equity ratio
B. return on equity
C. inventory turnover ratio
D. current ratio
Correct (D): The current ratio compares current assets to current liabilities to assess short-term liquidity.
A — why plausible but wrong: The debt-to-equity ratio measures overall leverage, not short-term liquidity specifically.

C. fixed cost
D. sunk cost

During peak season, a factory supervisor discovers that skipping a required safety inspection step would
allow the line to produce 15 percent more units per day. The best course of action is to
B159  |  Business Management & Administration  |  Operations  |  Judgment  |  Hard  |  Answer: A
A. maintain the required inspection to protect worker safety and product quality
B. skip the inspection temporarily since output is the priority
C. let each worker decide individually whether to follow the inspection step
D. postpone all inspections until after peak season ends
Correct (A): Safety inspections protect workers and product integrity; skipping them for short-term output gains creates
unacceptable risk.
B — why plausible but wrong: Skipping safety steps for higher output risks injuries and defective products, outweighing
short-term gains.
C — why plausible but wrong: Allowing inconsistent individual choices undermines safety standards and creates uneven risk.
D — why plausible but wrong: Postponing inspections during the busiest period is when risk of accidents and defects is
highest.

B202  |  Business Management & Administration  |  Project Management  |  Judgment  |  Hard  |  Answer: B

B. escalate the conflict to a senior manager who can prioritize based on overall business impact
C. have both projects proceed simultaneously despite the technician's limited time
D. cancel the lower-priority project entirely without further discussion
Correct (B): Escalating a resource conflict to a manager who can weigh overall business priorities ensures a fair, informed
resolution.
A — why plausible but wrong: Leaving the decision to the technician without organizational guidance could lead to a poor
prioritization decision.

D — why plausible but wrong: Canceling a project outright without evaluating alternatives is an extreme and premature
response.

What term describes a legally binding agreement between two or more parties that involves an offer,
acceptance, and consideration?
F001  |  Finance  |  Business Law  |  Recall  |  Easy  |  Answer: C
A. Warranty
B. Memorandum
C. Contract
D. Prospectus
Correct (C): A contract is a legally enforceable agreement formed when one party makes an offer, another accepts, and
something of value is exchanged.
A — why plausible but wrong: A warranty is a promise about the quality of a product, not the entire agreement itself.
B — why plausible but wrong: A memorandum is an informal written note, not necessarily a legally binding agreement.
D — why plausible but wrong: A prospectus is a disclosure document for investors, not a general binding agreement.
A bank teller notices that a customer is structuring several cash deposits under $10,000 to avoid federal
reporting requirements. This activity is best described as an attempt to evade which type of regulation?
F002  |  Finance  |  Business Law  |  Understanding  |  Medium  |  Answer: D
A. Truth in Lending disclosures
B. Fair Debt Collection limits
C. Equal Credit Opportunity rules
D. Anti-money-laundering reporting
Correct (D): Structuring deposits to stay below the reporting threshold is a classic attempt to avoid anti-money-laundering
currency transaction reporting.
A — why plausible but wrong: Truth in Lending concerns disclosure of loan terms, not cash deposit reporting.
B — why plausible but wrong: Fair Debt Collection limits govern how collectors contact debtors, unrelated to deposit
structuring.
C — why plausible but wrong: Equal Credit Opportunity rules address fair access to credit, not deposit reporting.
Which form of business ownership exposes the owner's personal assets to claims against the business?
F003  |  Finance  |  Business Law  |  Understanding  |  Easy  |  Answer: B
A. C corporation
B. Sole proprietorship
C. S corporation
D. Limited liability company
Correct (B): In a sole proprietorship, there is no legal separation between the owner and the business, so personal assets can
be used to satisfy business debts.
A — why plausible but wrong: A C corporation is a separate legal entity that generally shields owners' personal assets.
C — why plausible but wrong: An S corporation also provides liability protection separate from its shareholders.
D — why plausible but wrong: An LLC is designed specifically to limit owners' personal liability.

A — why plausible but wrong: Sarbanes-Oxley addresses corporate financial reporting and auditing, not consumer credit

B — why plausible but wrong: The Securities Exchange Act regulates trading of securities, not individual loan decisions.
C — why plausible but wrong: The Uniform Commercial Code governs commercial transactions broadly but does not require
adverse action notices.

A. Capacity
B. Legality
C. Consideration
D. Genuine assent
Correct (C): Consideration is something of value exchanged between parties; the employee's early completion of training is the
value given in exchange for the promised bonus.
A — why plausible but wrong: Capacity refers to the legal ability of parties to enter a contract, not the value exchanged.
B — why plausible but wrong: Legality concerns whether the contract's purpose is lawful, not the exchanged value.
D — why plausible but wrong: Genuine assent means both parties agreed willingly, which is separate from the value
exchanged.

D. Bank Secrecy Act

consumers can compare credit offers.
B — why plausible but wrong: The Community Reinvestment Act encourages banks to meet the credit needs of their
communities, not disclosure of loan terms.
C — why plausible but wrong: The Gramm-Leach-Bliley Act addresses financial privacy protections, not loan cost disclosures.
D — why plausible but wrong: The Bank Secrecy Act focuses on preventing money laundering through recordkeeping and
reporting.

than code authorship protection.

for damages.
D — why plausible but wrong: Rescission cancels the contract entirely rather than compensating for losses already incurred.

an important financial transaction.
A — why plausible but wrong: A voicemail is not a permanent written record and could be missed or unclear.

When a financial advisor repeats back a client's concerns in her own words to confirm understanding, she is
demonstrating
F021  |  Finance  |  Communications  |  Understanding  |  Easy  |  Answer: A
A. active listening
B. selective hearing
C. passive communication
D. closed questioning

B — why plausible but wrong: Selective hearing means only paying attention to certain parts of a message, which is the

C — why plausible but wrong: Passive communication involves failing to express oneself clearly, not confirming another's
message.
D — why plausible but wrong: Closed questioning refers to asking questions with limited yes/no answers, not restating what
was heard.

memo.

D. cross-selling strategy
Correct (A): Listening calmly, apologizing, and investigating the issue before responding are hallmarks of effective, professional

complaint.
C — why plausible but wrong: Conflict avoidance would mean ignoring or dismissing the issue, not researching and
responding to it.
D — why plausible but wrong: Cross-selling promotes related products, which does not describe handling this fee dispute.

When the demand for home loans increases while the supply of loanable funds stays the same, what

A. Interest rates increase

D. Interest rates decrease
Correct (A): When demand for loans rises without a matching increase in the supply of funds, the price of borrowing, or interest
rate, tends to rise.
B — why plausible but wrong: Negative rates are rare and not a typical outcome of a simple demand increase.
C — why plausible but wrong: Interest rates in most markets are not fixed by law but respond to market forces.
D — why plausible but wrong: Rates fall when demand decreases or supply increases, not when demand alone rises.
A sustained increase in the general price level of goods and services over time is called
F046  |  Finance  |  Economics  |  Recall  |  Easy  |  Answer: D
A. deflation
B. appreciation
C. stagnation
D. inflation
Correct (D): Inflation refers to a general and sustained rise in prices across an economy over time.
A — why plausible but wrong: Deflation is a decrease in the general price level, the opposite of inflation.
B — why plausible but wrong: Appreciation typically refers to an increase in the value of a specific asset or currency, not
general prices.
C — why plausible but wrong: Stagnation refers to slow or no economic growth, not specifically rising prices.

F047  |  Finance  |  Economics  |  Understanding  |  Medium  |  Answer: B
A. trade policy
B. monetary policy
C. labor policy
D. fiscal policy

C — why plausible but wrong: Labor policy addresses employment regulations, unrelated to central bank rate decisions.

rate actions.
A small business owner decides to invest extra cash in new equipment instead of paying down a loan early.
The interest savings she gives up by not paying down the loan represent her
F048  |  Finance  |  Economics  |  Application  |  Hard  |  Answer: A
A. opportunity cost
B. fixed cost
C. marginal cost
D. sunk cost
Correct (A): Opportunity cost is the value of the next best alternative given up when a choice is made, such as the interest
savings foregone.
B — why plausible but wrong: A fixed cost remains constant regardless of production level, unrelated to this decision.
C — why plausible but wrong: Marginal cost is the cost of producing one additional unit, not the value of a foregone choice.
D — why plausible but wrong: A sunk cost is money already spent that cannot be recovered, not a forgone alternative.

A company sells a product for $50 per unit, with variable costs of $30 per unit and fixed costs of $40,000.
How many units must the company sell to break even?
F098  |  Finance  |  Financial Analysis  |  Application  |  Hard  |  Answer: A
A. 2,000 units
B. 1,000 units
C. 800 units
D. 1,333 units
Correct (A): Break-even units equal fixed costs divided by contribution margin per unit ($50 - $30 = $20); $40,000 divided by
$20 equals 2,000 units.
B — why plausible but wrong: This figure underestimates the number of units needed based on the given costs.
C — why plausible but wrong: This figure results from an incorrect contribution margin calculation.
D — why plausible but wrong: This figure results from dividing fixed costs by the sales price instead of the contribution margin.

D — why plausible but wrong: Dividend amounts can change over time and are not fixed like a bond coupon rate.

Correct (A): As an amortizing loan is repaid, the proportion of each payment applied to principal increases while the interest

C — why plausible but wrong: Standard amortizing loans include both principal and interest throughout most of the term, not
principal alone.

A. 2.0
B. 2.5
C. 1.0
D. 1.5
Correct (D): The quick ratio equals current assets minus inventory, divided by current liabilities: ($200,000 - $50,000) / $100,000
= 1.5.
A — why plausible but wrong: This figure matches the current ratio, not the quick ratio, which excludes inventory.
B — why plausible but wrong: This figure overstates the ratio beyond what the adjusted asset calculation supports.
C — why plausible but wrong: This value would only result if inventory were not subtracted at all, which is incorrect for the
quick ratio.

A bookkeeper discovers that a $500 expense was accidentally recorded as $5,000 in the accounting system.
What is the most appropriate next step?
F151  |  Finance  |  Financial-Information Management  |  Application  |  Medium  |  Answer: D
A. Delete all entries from that day to start over
B. Leave the error uncorrected since it will balance out eventually
C. Report the error only if a customer complains
D. Make a correcting entry to accurately reflect the actual expense
Correct (D): Financial errors should be promptly corrected with an adjusting entry to ensure the records accurately reflect actual
transactions.
A — why plausible but wrong: Deleting all entries for the day is unnecessary and could remove valid transactions.
B — why plausible but wrong: Errors of this size will not simply balance out and must be corrected.
C — why plausible but wrong: Errors should be corrected proactively, not only after a complaint is received.

The main purpose of preparing a trial balance is to verify that
F153  |  Finance  |  Financial-Information Management  |  Recall  |  Medium  |  Answer: C
A. all customers have paid their invoices
B. the company has sufficient cash on hand
C. total debits equal total credits in the ledger
D. the company earned a profit for the period
Correct (C): A trial balance checks that the sum of all debit balances equals the sum of all credit balances, helping detect
recording errors.
A — why plausible but wrong: Customer payment status is unrelated to the trial balance's purpose.
B — why plausible but wrong: Cash sufficiency is assessed through cash flow analysis, not the trial balance.
D — why plausible but wrong: Profitability is determined by the income statement, not the trial balance itself.
A company's accounts receivable ledger shows that several customer invoices are more than 90 days past
due. What should the finance team do first with this information?
F154  |  Finance  |  Financial-Information Management  |  Application  |  Hard  |  Answer: C
A. Ignore the invoices until the next fiscal year
B. Automatically write off all overdue invoices as bad debt
C. Review the aging report and follow up with those customers
D. Immediately terminate service to all customers involved
Correct (C): Reviewing the accounts receivable aging report and following up with overdue customers is the appropriate first
step before deciding on further action.
A — why plausible but wrong: Ignoring overdue accounts risks further financial loss and should not be delayed.
B — why plausible but wrong: Writing off debt immediately skips necessary collection efforts and verification.
D — why plausible but wrong: Terminating service abruptly, without follow-up, is an extreme first response.

B. trial balance

B — why plausible but wrong: A trial balance summarizes ledger balances, not an original transaction record.
C — why plausible but wrong: A financial forecast predicts future performance, not evidence of a past transaction.
D — why plausible but wrong: A budget variance report compares actual to budgeted amounts, not an original transaction
document.

H052  |  Hospitality & Tourism  |  Economics  |  Analysis  |  Hard  |  Answer: A

Page 305
12. Limitations & Self-Critique Notes
  Not affiliated. These items are original practice content modeled on public DECA exam style. They are not official
DECA or MBA Research items, are not endorsed by either organization, and should not be represented as authentic
exam questions.
  Fidelity claim is bounded. "DECA-style" fidelity is calibrated to recurring patterns in publicly available sample
exams and the published blueprint. Official secure exam forms were not accessed; private item-bank content was not
used.
  Allocation rounded. Per-cluster counts were scaled from the 100-item district blueprint to ~250 items and
rounded; a few areas deviate by one or two items from the strict proportional target so that every instructional area
meets its minimum coverage for templating.
  Cognitive mix slightly enriched. Analysis/judgment run modestly above the ~5% target in some clusters to
provide variety for the downstream 40,000-item expansion; conceptual and application shares remain dominant as in
authentic exams.
  Distractor plausibility is heuristic. Distractor rationales target documented common student misconceptions but
have not been field-tested for empirical discrimination; a pilot administration would be needed to compute real
item-difficulty and discrimination indices.
  Originality. Stems, options, and named characters were generated from scratch; a final deduplication pass
confirmed no repeated stems and no verbatim reuse of official wording from the cited public samples.
Sources
1. MBA Research and Curriculum Center, DECA Exam Specifications 2024–2025.
https://www.mbaresearch.org/wp-content/uploads/2024/09/Exam-Specifications-2024-25.pdf
2. MBA Research and Curriculum Center, Marketing Career Cluster sample exam (posted for DECA).
https://gtowndeca.weebly.com/uploads/3/1/4/3/31437899/hs_marketing_cluster_sample_exam.pdf
3. BHS DECA, Team Decision Making practice set. http://www.bhsdeca.org/uploads/1/1/0/1/110157709/2018_tdm_set.pdf
4. Enloe DECA, Practice tests by cluster. https://enloehsdeca.weebly.com/practice-tests.html
5. DECA Direct, A Student Guide to Mastering DECA's Role-plays.
https://www.decadirect.org/articles/a-student-guide-to-mastering-decas-role-plays
6. decaplus.org, Online Practice Exams. https://www.decaplus.org/access/exams
7. DECA Inc., Performance Indicators (Hospitality & Tourism) and the public DECA Performance Indicators (PIs) List.
https://www.deca.org/advisor-resources/performance-indicators-hospitality-and-tourism
